# Graph API overview

> Learn how LangGraph models agent workflows as graphs of shared state, nodes, and edges.

The Graph API defines agent workflows as a graph of [state](#state), [nodes](#nodes), and [edges](#edges). [`Send`](#send) and [`Command`](#command) control how the graph runs work: `Send` dynamically invokes nodes for fan-out, and `Command` updates state while routing to the next node (including resume after an interrupt). Use the Graph API when you want explicit shared state and a visual control flow.

For step-by-step decisions while building, see the [Build checklist for the Graph API](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/use-graph-api.md). For a guided walkthrough, see [Thinking in the Graph API](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/thinking-in-graph-api.md).

## Graphs

LangGraph models agent workflows as graphs built from three parts:

1. [`State`](#state): A shared snapshot of the application. Usually a typed schema.
2. [`Nodes`](#nodes): Functions that read state, do work, and return updates.
3. [`Edges`](#edges): Fixed or conditional transitions that determine the next node.

Compose nodes and edges to build sequences, branches, and loops. Nodes and edges are ordinary functions. They can call an LLM or run other code.

In short: nodes do the work; edges decide what runs next.

### Super-steps

LangGraph runs graphs with [message passing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Message_passing), in the style of Google's [Pregel](https://research.google/pubs/pregel-a-system-for-large-scale-graph-processing/) system. Execution advances in discrete **super-steps**:

* Nodes that run in parallel share one super-step.
* Nodes that run in sequence use separate super-steps.
* A node becomes active when it receives input on an incoming edge.
* After a super-step, nodes with no pending messages become inactive.
* The run ends when every node is inactive and no messages remain in transit.

### StateGraph

[`StateGraph`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/graph/state/StateGraph) is the main graph builder. Parameterize it with your state schema.

### Compiling your graph

Build a graph by defining [state](#state), adding [nodes](#nodes) and [edges](#edges), then compiling. Compilation checks the graph structure (for example for orphaned nodes). It is also where you attach runtime options such as [checkpointers](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/persistence.md) and breakpoints.

You must compile the graph before you invoke or stream it. This is the smallest end-to-end example:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from typing_extensions import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class State(TypedDict):
    text: str


def node_a(state: State):
    return {"text": state["text"] + "a"}


graph = (
    StateGraph(State)
    .add_node("node_a", node_a)
    .add_edge(START, "node_a")
    .add_edge("node_a", END)
    .compile()
)

graph.invoke({"text": ""})
# {'text': 'a'}
```

## State

State is the shared data that nodes read and update. LangGraph stores that data in **channels**: named slots in the state schema (the keys of your state object). Each channel holds a value and defines how updates merge into it. Most channels use last-write-wins. Channels with a [reducer](#reducers) combine values instead, for example by appending to a list.

State has two parts:

* A [schema](#schema) that declares the channels in the graph
* Optional [reducers](#reducers) that control how updates merge into each channel

Nodes return partial updates. LangGraph applies those updates through each channel's reducer.

Define state with a [`TypedDict`](https://docs.python.org/3/library/typing.html#typing.TypedDict), a [`dataclass`](https://docs.python.org/3/library/dataclasses.html) (for defaults), or a Pydantic [`BaseModel`](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-state#use-pydantic-models-for-graph-state) when you need validation. Prefer `TypedDict` or `dataclass` for performance.

> [!NOTE]
>
> [`create_agent`](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langchain/agents) state schemas must be `TypedDict`. Pydantic models are not supported.

### Schema

By default, the graph input and output schemas match the full state. You can narrow them when some keys are internal only. See [Define input and output schemas](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-state.md#define-input-and-output-schemas).

#### Multiple schemas

Most graphs share one state schema. Use multiple schemas when you need either of the following:

* Private channels that internal nodes share, but that are not part of graph input or output
* Explicit input and output schemas that are subsets of the full internal state

A node can write to any channel in the graph state, including channels outside its declared input schema. Nodes can also introduce private channels when those schemas are defined. Input and output schemas constrain what `invoke` accepts and returns. They do not hide channels from [streaming](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/streaming.md) by default. See [Private channels and streaming](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-state.md#private-channels-and-streaming).

For examples, see [Define input and output schemas](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-state.md#define-input-and-output-schemas) and [Pass private state between nodes](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-state.md#pass-private-state-between-nodes).

### Reducers

Reducers control how node updates merge into state. Each key has its own reducer. With no reducer, updates overwrite the previous value.

#### Reducer arguments

A reducer is a binary function:

* **Left**: The current value in state for that key
* **Right**: The update returned by a node for that key

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
new_value = reducer(left=current_state[key], right=node_update[key])
```

Define a reducer and attach it to a state key with `Annotated`:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from typing import Annotated

from typing_extensions import TypedDict


def append_strings(left: list[str], right: list[str]) -> list[str]:
    """Combine the existing state value (left) with a node update (right)."""
    return left + right


class State(TypedDict):
    tags: Annotated[list[str], append_strings]
```

Calling the reducer with left and right shows how values combine:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
append_strings(left=["draft"], right=["review"])  # returns ["draft", "review"]
```

#### Default reducer

The default reducer replaces the left value with the right. Nodes return partial updates, not the full state.

For example, start with `{"foo": 1, "bar": ["hi"]}`. An update of `{"foo": 2}` yields `{"foo": 2, "bar": ["hi"]}`. A later `{"bar": ["bye"]}` yields `{"foo": 2, "bar": ["bye"]}`.

#### Custom reducers

Use a custom reducer to combine values, such as appending to a list.

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
import operator
from typing import Annotated

from typing_extensions import TypedDict


class State(TypedDict):
    foo: int
    bar: Annotated[list[str], operator.add]
```

Here `foo` overwrites. `bar` appends with `operator.add`. From `{"foo": 1, "bar": ["hi"]}`, an update of `{"bar": ["bye"]}` becomes `{"foo": 1, "bar": ["hi", "bye"]}`.

#### Overwrite

To bypass a merging reducer and replace a value (including clearing it), use [`Overwrite`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/types/). See [Bypass reducers with Overwrite](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-state.md#bypass-reducers-with-overwrite).

#### Resetting a reducer field

With a merging reducer, returning an empty value keeps prior data. That matters for error buffers or retry counters that must clear between attempts. See [Resetting a reducer field](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-state.md#resetting-a-reducer-field) and [Bypass reducers with Overwrite](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-state.md#bypass-reducers-with-overwrite).

### Working with messages in graph state

#### Why use messages?

Chat models take a list of messages as input. LangChain's [chat model interface](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langchain/models.md) accepts message objects such as [`HumanMessage`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langchain-core/messages/human/HumanMessage) and [`AIMessage`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langchain-core/messages/ai/AIMessage). See the [Messages conceptual guide](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langchain/messages.md).

#### Using messages in your graph

Store conversation history as a list of messages in state, with a reducer that appends new messages and updates existing ones by ID.

Use [`add_messages`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/graph/message/add_messages) on a `messages` key. Plain `operator.add` only appends, so it cannot update an existing message in place during human-in-the-loop edits.

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from typing import Annotated

from langchain.messages import AnyMessage
from langgraph.graph.message import add_messages
from typing_extensions import TypedDict


class GraphState(TypedDict):
    messages: Annotated[list[AnyMessage], add_messages]
```

#### Serialization

Message reducers also deserialize common wire formats into LangChain message objects.

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
# Supported
{"messages": [HumanMessage(content="message")]}

# Also supported
{"messages": [{"type": "human", "content": "message"}]}
```

Access attributes with dot notation, for example `state["messages"][-1].content`. See [LangChain serialization](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langchain/messages.md#serialization).

#### MessagesState

`MessagesState` is a prebuilt state with a single `messages` key that uses [`add_messages`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/graph/message/add_messages). Subclass it to add fields:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from langgraph.graph import MessagesState


class State(MessagesState):
    documents: list[str]
```

## Nodes

Nodes are sync or async functions that implement a step of the graph.

A node can accept:

1. `state`: The graph [state](#state)
2. `config`: A [`RunnableConfig`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langchain-core/runnables/config/RunnableConfig) with values such as `thread_id` and `tags`
3. `runtime`: A `Runtime` object with [runtime context](#runtime-context), `store`, `stream_writer`, `execution_info`, and related helpers

Add nodes with [`add_node`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/graph/state/StateGraph/add_node):

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from dataclasses import dataclass

from langgraph.graph import StateGraph
from langgraph.runtime import Runtime
from typing_extensions import TypedDict


class State(TypedDict):
    input: str
    results: str


@dataclass
class Context:
    user_id: str


builder = StateGraph(State)


def plain_node(state: State):
    return state


def node_with_runtime(state: State, runtime: Runtime[Context]):
    return {"results": f"Hello, {state['input']}!"}


builder.add_node("plain_node", plain_node)
builder.add_node("node_with_runtime", node_with_runtime)
```

Behind the scenes, LangGraph wraps functions as [`RunnableLambda`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langchain-core/runnables/base/RunnableLambda) so they gain batching, async support, and [tracing](https://docs.langchain.com/langsmith/observability.md).

If you omit a node name, LangGraph uses the function name.

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
builder.add_node(my_node)
# Reference the node as "my_node" in edges
```

### Re-execution and idempotency

With a [checkpointer](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/persistence.md), LangGraph saves checkpoints at [super-step](#super-steps) boundaries, not mid-function inside a node. After an [interrupt](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/interrupts.md) or [retry](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/fault-tolerance.md#retries), the affected node runs again from the start of its function. Side effects before the pause run again.

Design node logic so re-execution is safe. Prefer idempotency keys, upserts, or read-before-write checks. For durable keys and external side effects under retries, see [Retry boundaries and idempotency](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/fault-tolerance.md#retry-boundaries-and-idempotency). For effects around `interrupt()`, see [Side effects called before `interrupt` must be idempotent](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/interrupts#side-effects-called-before-interrupt-must-be-idempotent).

You can add or remove nodes and edges without breaking resume for existing threads. Resumed runs use saved state and the graph you compile now.

If a node calls [tasks](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/functional-api.md#task) or [`interrupt`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/types/interrupt), stricter [determinism](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/functional-api.md#determinism) rules apply on resume. Completed task results are restored from the checkpointer. Changing task or interrupt order before the resume point can mismatch cached values. See [Idempotency](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/functional-api.md#idempotency).

### Choose the unit of work

A [node](#nodes) is a checkpoint and retry boundary. It is also where the graph can take a different path. Deterministic work such as parsing, retrieval, or a fixed tool chain does not need its own node unless you want that boundary.

Ask whether the step decides what happens next, or executes something already decided. Then pick the primitive that matches the recovery behavior you need:

| Primitive | Own checkpoint | On resume or retry | Choose when |
| - | - | - | - |
| Plain function inside a node | No. It runs inside the node's [super-step](#super-steps). | Re-runs whenever the node re-runs. | No routing fork after the step, and repeating it with the rest of the node is acceptable. |
| [Task](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/functional-api.md#task) | No new graph checkpoint. The result is stored with the running entrypoint or parent checkpoint. | The enclosing entrypoint or node replays from the start. Completed tasks return stored results. | The step has a side effect or is expensive, and must not repeat on resume. |
| [Subgraph](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/use-subgraphs.md) | Yes for stateful modes: nodes checkpoint under a namespace of the parent thread. Compile with `checkpointer=False` for no subgraph checkpoints. | Stateful modes resume inside the subgraph at the interrupted or failed node. | The unit has its own routing or state schema, or more than one parent graph reuses it. |
| Graph node | Yes. A checkpoint follows the [super-step](#super-steps) it runs in. | Resume starts at that node. Earlier nodes do not re-run. | A routing fork is possible after the step, or you need an interrupt, resume, or [time travel](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/use-time-travel.md) boundary. |

For side effects under retries, see [Retry boundaries and idempotency](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/fault-tolerance.md#retry-boundaries-and-idempotency). For task retries and timeouts, see [Fault tolerance](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/fault-tolerance.md).

### Using tasks in nodes

When one node runs several operations that should checkpoint independently, wrap each operation in a [task](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/functional-api.md#task) instead of splitting the work across nodes. With a checkpointer, completed task results are saved, so a resumed thread can skip finished work inside the node. For patterns and examples, see [Tasks](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/functional-api.md#task) and [Common pitfalls](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/functional-api.md#common-pitfalls).

### `START` node

[`START`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/constants/START) is the virtual entry that delivers user input into the graph. Use it to mark which nodes run first.

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from langgraph.graph import START

graph.add_edge(START, "node_a")
```

### `END` node

`END` marks a terminal edge. Use it when a path should stop.

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from langgraph.graph import END

graph.add_edge("node_a", END)
```

### Node caching

LangGraph can cache node (and task) results by input. Pass a cache when you compile the graph, and attach a [`CachePolicy`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/types/CachePolicy) on the node.

For options, end-to-end examples, and graph-wide defaults, see [Add node caching](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-nodes.md#add-node-caching).

## Edges

Edges decide where control goes next:

* **Normal edges**: Always go from one node to the next
* **Conditional edges**: Call a function to determine the next node(s)
* **Entry point**: First node after user input (`START` → node)
* **Conditional entry point**: Route from `START` with a function

A node can have multiple outgoing edges. Destinations on those edges run in parallel in the next super-step.

> [!WARNING]
>
> For each node, use either static edges or dynamic routing ([conditional edges](#conditional-edges) / [`Command`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/types/Command)), not both. Mixing them can run both paths.

### Normal edges

If you always want to go from node A to node B, use [`add_edge`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/pregel/_draw/add_edge):

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
graph.add_edge("node_a", "node_b")
```

### Conditional edges

If you want to optionally route to one or more nodes (or terminate), use [`add_conditional_edges`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/graph/state/StateGraph/add_conditional_edges). Pass the source node and a routing function to call after that node runs:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
graph.add_conditional_edges("node_a", routing_function)
# Or with an explicit map:
graph.add_conditional_edges(
    "node_a",
    routing_function,
    {"path_a": "node_b", "path_b": "node_c"},
)
```

The routing function receives the current graph state. By default, its return value is the name of the next node (or a list of names). Those nodes run in parallel in the next super-step. You can also pass a dictionary that maps the function's output to node names.

The routing function can return a node name, `END`, a list of names (fan-out), or [`Send`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/types/Send) objects for map-reduce. See [Build sequences, branches, and loops](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-control-flow.md).

> [!TIP]
>
> Use [`Command`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/types/Command) instead of conditional edges when you want to combine state updates and routing in a single function.

### Entry point

The entry point is the first node (or nodes) that run when the graph starts. Add an edge from the virtual [`START`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/constants/START) node to the first node to execute:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
graph.add_edge(START, "node_a")
```

### Conditional entry point

A conditional entry point lets you start at different nodes depending on custom logic. Use [`add_conditional_edges`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/graph/state/StateGraph/add_conditional_edges) from [`START`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/constants/START):

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
graph.add_conditional_edges(START, routing_function)
```

You can optionally provide a dictionary that maps the routing function's output to the next node name.

## `Send`

Use [`Send`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/types/Send) when you need to run the same node once per item, each with its own input, and the number of items is only known at runtime. That shape is often called map-reduce or orchestrator-worker: one node produces a list, workers process each item in parallel, then a later node reads the combined results.

Normal edges cannot do this. They choose which nodes run next, but every chosen node still receives the shared graph state, and the topology is fixed when you build the graph.

Return one or more `Send` objects from a conditional edge. Each `Send` names a target node and passes a custom argument as that invocation's input. LangGraph runs those invocations in parallel. When workers write to shared state keys, [reducers](#reducers) merge their updates, for example by concatenating lists.

The first argument to `Send` is the target node name. The second is the state (or partial state) for that invocation.

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from langgraph.types import Send


def continue_to_jokes(state: OverallState):
    return [Send("generate_joke", {"subject": s}) for s in state["subjects"]]


graph.add_conditional_edges("node_a", continue_to_jokes)
```

For a full map-reduce example, see [Use Send for map-reduce](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-send-command.md#use-send-for-map-reduce).

## `Command`

Use [`Command`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/types/Command) when a single step must both update state and choose what runs next. Normally those jobs are separate: a node returns state updates, and [edges](#edges) (or [conditional edges](#conditional-edges)) decide the next node. Return `Command` from a node when the next hop depends on work you just did in that same function.

`Command` also covers two other cases that plain returns and edges do not: resume a run after an [interrupt](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/interrupts.md), and update or route from inside a [tool](#return-from-tools).

For routing alone, prefer conditional edges. Reach for `Command` when you need the update and the route together (or one of the interrupt / tool cases above).

`Command` accepts:

* **`update`**: State updates (like a normal node return)
* **`goto`**: Next node(s) (like a conditional edge)
* **`graph`**: Parent graph target when navigating from a [subgraph](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/use-subgraphs.md)
* **`resume`**: Value used to resume after an [interrupt](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/interrupts.md)

Use `Command` in three places:

* **[Return from nodes](#return-from-nodes)**: `update`, `goto`, and `graph`
* **[Input to `invoke` or `stream`](#input-to-invoke-or-stream)**: `resume` after an interrupt
* **[Return from tools](#return-from-tools)**: Updates and routing from inside a tool

### Return from nodes

#### `update` and `goto`

Return [`Command`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/types/Command) to update state and route in one step:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
def my_node(state: CommandState) -> Command[Literal["my_other_node"]]:
    return Command(
        update={"foo": "bar"},
        goto="my_other_node",
    )
```

Annotate the return type with the allowed destinations, for example `Command[Literal["my_other_node"]]`. That tells LangGraph which edges to render.

Use [`Command`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/types/Command) when you need both an update and a route. For routing alone, prefer [conditional edges](#conditional-edges).

> [!WARNING]
>
> `Command` adds dynamic edges. Static edges from the same node still run. Do not mix `Command` routing with `add_edge` from one node.

See [Combine control flow and state updates with Command](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-send-command.md#combine-control-flow-and-state-updates-with-command).

#### `graph`

From a [subgraph](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/use-subgraphs.md) node, navigate to a node in the parent graph with `Command.PARENT`:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
def my_node(state: CommandState) -> Command[Literal["other_subgraph"]]:
    return Command(
        update={"foo": "bar"},
        goto="other_subgraph",  # where `other_subgraph` is a node in the parent graph
        graph=Command.PARENT,
    )
```

When you update a parent key that the subgraph also uses, define a [reducer](#reducers) for that key in the parent. See [Navigate to a node in a parent graph](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-send-command.md#navigate-to-a-node-in-a-parent-graph).

### Input to `invoke` or `stream`

> [!WARNING]
>
> `Command(resume=...)` is the only `Command` pattern meant as input to `invoke()` / `stream()` (optionally with `update=...` while resuming). Do not pass `Command(update=...)` alone to continue a conversation. Any `Command` as input resumes from the latest checkpoint, not from `START`, so a finished thread can look stuck. Pass a plain dict to start a new turn:
>
> ```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
> # Wrong: resumes from the latest checkpoint
> graph.invoke(Command(update={  # [!code --]
>     "messages": [{"role": "user", "content": "follow up"}]  # [!code --]
> }), config)  # [!code --]
>
> # Correct: plain dict restarts from START
> graph.invoke({  # [!code ++]
>     "messages": [{"role": "user", "content": "follow up"}]  # [!code ++]
> }, config)  # [!code ++]
> ```

#### `resume`

Use `Command(resume=...)` after an [interrupt](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/interrupts.md). The value becomes the return value of the paused `interrupt()` call:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
graph.invoke(Command(resume="yes"), config)
```

See [Interrupts](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/interrupts.md) for full patterns.

### Return from tools

Return [`Command`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/types/Command) from tools to update state and route after the tool finishes. `goto` from a tool adds a dynamic edge. Static edges on the calling node still run, so do not mix both.

See [Use inside tools](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-send-command.md#use-inside-tools).

## Graph migrations

You can change a graph's nodes, edges, and state schema even when a [checkpointer](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/persistence.md) already stores thread state. What is safe depends on whether the thread has finished or is paused.

**Topology (nodes and edges)**

* Finished threads (not interrupted): you can add, remove, or rename nodes and edges freely.
* Interrupted threads: you can change edges and add nodes. Do not rename or remove a node the thread is about to enter. On resume, LangGraph looks up that node by name, and a missing name fails the run.

**State schema**

* Adding or removing keys is backwards and forwards compatible.
* Renaming a key drops the saved value for that key in existing threads.
* Incompatible type changes on an existing key (for example, narrowing or requiring a field that old checkpoints omit) can break older thread state.

For logic changes that stay schema-compatible (for example, a new tool set), see [Business compatibility](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/backward-compatibility.md#business-compatibility).

## Runtime context

Runtime context passes dependencies into nodes without putting them in graph state (for example, model name or a database handle).

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from dataclasses import dataclass

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph
from langgraph.runtime import Runtime
from typing_extensions import TypedDict


class State(TypedDict):
    input: str
    output: str


@dataclass
class ContextSchema:
    llm_provider: str = "openai"


def get_llm(provider: str) -> str:
    return provider


def node_a(state: State, runtime: Runtime[ContextSchema]):
    llm = get_llm(runtime.context.llm_provider)
    return {"output": llm}


graph = (
    StateGraph(State, context_schema=ContextSchema)
    .add_node("node_a", node_a)
    .add_edge(START, "node_a")
    .add_edge("node_a", END)
    .compile()
)
graph.invoke({"input": "hi"}, context={"llm_provider": "anthropic"})
```

See [Add runtime configuration](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-nodes.md#add-runtime-configuration).

### Recursion limit

The recursion limit caps how many [super-steps](#super-steps) one run may execute. When the limit is exceeded, LangGraph raises `GraphRecursionError`.

Starting in version 1.1.4, the default is 10,007 super-steps. Between 1.0.6 and 1.1.3, the default is 10,000 steps. Override it with the `LANGGRAPH_DEFAULT_RECURSION_LIMIT` environment variable, or pass `recursion_limit` as a top-level config key (not under `configurable`).

For recipes (catching the error, reading `langgraph_step`, and Python `RemainingSteps`), see [Impose a recursion limit](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-control-flow.md#impose-a-recursion-limit) and [Access and handle the recursion counter](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-control-flow.md#access-and-handle-the-recursion-counter).

# Define and update state

> Define graph state schemas, reducers, messages, and private state in the LangGraph Graph API.

[State](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api.md#state) is the shared schema that nodes read and write. [Reducers](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api.md#reducers) control how each update merges into the current value. This page covers:

1. How to use state to define a graph's [schema](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api.md#schema)
2. How to use reducers to control how state updates are processed

## Define state

[State](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api.md#state) in LangGraph can be a `TypedDict`, `Pydantic` model, or dataclass. The examples below use `TypedDict`. Prefer that form for most graphs; see [Use Pydantic models for graph state](#use-pydantic-models-for-graph-state) when you need runtime validation on inputs.

By default, a graph uses the same schema for input and output, and the state determines that schema. See [Define input and output schemas](#define-input-and-output-schemas) for distinct input and output schemas.

Start with a [messages](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api.md#working-with-messages-in-graph-state) field. That shape fits many LLM applications. For more detail, see [Working with messages in graph state](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api.md#working-with-messages-in-graph-state).

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from langchain.messages import AnyMessage
from typing_extensions import TypedDict


class State(TypedDict):
    messages: list[AnyMessage]
    extra_field: int
```

This state tracks a list of [message](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langchain/messages.md) objects and an extra integer field. There is no reducer yet, so a node that updates `messages` must return the full list.

## Update state

Build a graph with a single [node](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api.md#nodes). The node is a Python function that reads graph state and returns updates. The first argument is always the state:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from langchain.messages import AIMessage


def node(state: State):
    messages = state["messages"]
    new_message = AIMessage("Hello!")
    return {"messages": messages + [new_message], "extra_field": 10}
```

This node appends a message by concatenating in the return value (no reducer yet) and sets `extra_field`.

> [!WARNING]
>
> Nodes should return updates to the state directly, instead of mutating the state.

Define a simple graph with this node. Use [`StateGraph`](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api#stategraph) for a graph that operates on this state, then [`add_node`](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api#nodes) to populate the graph:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from langgraph.graph import StateGraph

builder = StateGraph(State)
builder.add_node(node)
builder.set_entry_point("node")
graph = builder.compile()
```

LangGraph provides built-in utilities for visualizing a graph. See [Visualize your graph](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api-visualize.md) for detail.

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from IPython.display import Image, display

display(Image(graph.get_graph().draw_mermaid_png()))
```

```mermaid theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
%%{init:{'theme':'base','themeVariables':{'lineColor':'#40668D','primaryColor':'#E5F4FF','primaryTextColor':'#030710','primaryBorderColor':'#006DDD'}}}%%
flowchart LR
    startNode([START]) --> n[node]

    classDef process fill:#E5F4FF,stroke:#006DDD,stroke-width:2px,color:#030710
    classDef terminal fill:#F2FAFF,stroke:#40668D,stroke-width:2px,color:#030710
    class n process
    class startNode terminal
```

This graph executes a single node. Invoke it:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from langchain.messages import HumanMessage

result = graph.invoke({"messages": [HumanMessage("Hi")]})
result
```

```
{'messages': [HumanMessage(content='Hi'), AIMessage(content='Hello!')], 'extra_field': 10}
```

Note that:

* Invocation started by updating a single key of the state.
* The invocation result includes the entire state.

Inspect [message](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langchain/messages.md) content with pretty-print:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
for message in result["messages"]:
    message.pretty_print()
```

```
================================ Human Message ================================

Hi
================================== Ai Message ==================================

Hello!
```

## Process state updates with reducers

Each key in the state can have its own [reducer](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api.md#reducers) function, which controls how updates from nodes are applied. If no reducer is specified, updates to that key replace the previous value.

For `TypedDict` state schemas, define reducers by annotating the field with a reducer function.

In the earlier example, the node updated `"messages"` by returning the full concatenated list. Add a reducer so updates are appended automatically:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from typing_extensions import Annotated


def add(left, right):
    """Can also import `add` from the `operator` built-in."""
    return left + right


class State(TypedDict):
    messages: Annotated[list[AnyMessage], add]  # [!code highlight]
    extra_field: int
```

The node can then return only the new message:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
def node(state: State):
    new_message = AIMessage("Hello!")
    return {"messages": [new_message], "extra_field": 10}  # [!code highlight]
```

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from langgraph.graph import START

graph = StateGraph(State).add_node(node).add_edge(START, "node").compile()

result = graph.invoke({"messages": [HumanMessage("Hi")]})

for message in result["messages"]:
    message.pretty_print()
```

```
================================ Human Message ================================

Hi
================================== Ai Message ==================================

Hello!
```

### MessagesState

In practice, lists of messages need more than plain concatenation:

* Update an existing message in the state by ID.
* Accept shorthand [message formats](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/graph-api.md#serialization), such as `{"role": "user", "content": "..."}`.

LangGraph includes a built-in reducer [`add_messages`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/graph/message/add_messages) that handles these cases:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from langgraph.graph.message import add_messages


class State(TypedDict):
    messages: Annotated[list[AnyMessage], add_messages]  # [!code highlight]
    extra_field: int


def node(state: State):
    new_message = AIMessage("Hello!")
    return {"messages": [new_message], "extra_field": 10}


graph = StateGraph(State).add_node(node).set_entry_point("node").compile()
```

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
input_message = {"role": "user", "content": "Hi"}  # [!code highlight]

result = graph.invoke({"messages": [input_message]})

for message in result["messages"]:
    message.pretty_print()
```

```
================================ Human Message ================================

Hi
================================== Ai Message ==================================

Hello!
```

This shape fits applications that use [chat models](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langchain/models.md). For convenience, subclass the prebuilt `MessagesState`:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from langgraph.graph import MessagesState


class State(MessagesState):
    extra_field: int
```

## Resetting a reducer field

A common source of confusion with reducers is that with a merging reducer, returning an empty value does not clear the field. The reducer merges the right argument into the left one, so an empty update is merged in and previously accumulated values are kept.

This pattern matters for error buffers or retry counters that must be cleared between retry attempts:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from operator import add
from typing import Annotated

from typing_extensions import TypedDict


class State(TypedDict):
    errors: Annotated[list[str], add]


# node A returns {"errors": ["bad sql"]}
# node B returns {"errors": []}
# state["errors"] is still ["bad sql"]; the empty list is merged in, not cleared
```

To clear the field while keeping a merging reducer, wrap the update with [`Overwrite`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/types/):

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from operator import add
from typing import Annotated

from langgraph.types import Overwrite
from typing_extensions import TypedDict


class State(TypedDict):
    errors: Annotated[list[str], add]


def clear_errors(state: State):
    # Bypass the merging reducer and clear the field
    return {"errors": Overwrite([])}
```

For more information, see [Bypass reducers with Overwrite](#bypass-reducers-with-overwrite).

## Bypass reducers with `Overwrite`

In some cases, you may want to bypass a reducer and directly overwrite a state value. When a node returns a value wrapped with `Overwrite`, the reducer is bypassed and the channel is set directly to that value.

This is useful when you want to reset or replace accumulated state rather than merge it with existing values.

LangGraph provides the [`Overwrite`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/types/) type for this purpose.

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
import operator

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph
from langgraph.types import Overwrite
from typing_extensions import Annotated, TypedDict


class State(TypedDict):
    messages: Annotated[list, operator.add]


def add_message(state: State):
    return {"messages": ["first message"]}


def replace_messages(state: State):
    # Bypass the reducer and replace the entire messages list
    return {"messages": Overwrite(["replacement message"])}


builder = StateGraph(State)
builder.add_node("add_message", add_message)
builder.add_node("replace_messages", replace_messages)
builder.add_edge(START, "add_message")
builder.add_edge("add_message", "replace_messages")
builder.add_edge("replace_messages", END)

graph = builder.compile()

result = graph.invoke({"messages": ["initial"]})
print(result["messages"])
```

```
['replacement message']
```

You can also use JSON format with the special key `"__overwrite__"`:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
def replace_messages(state: State):
    return {"messages": {"__overwrite__": ["replacement message"]}}
```

> [!WARNING]
>
> When nodes execute in parallel, only one node can use `Overwrite` on the same state key in a given super-step. If multiple nodes attempt to overwrite the same key in the same super-step, an `InvalidUpdateError` will be raised. An `Overwrite` also discards regular updates that other nodes write to the same key in that super-step. The key ends up with only the `Overwrite` value.

## Define input and output schemas

By default, `StateGraph` operates with a single schema, and all nodes communicate using that schema. You can also define distinct input and output schemas.

When distinct schemas are specified, an internal schema is still used for communication between nodes. The input schema validates the provided input. The output schema filters the internal data so `invoke` returns only the fields in the output schema.

Define distinct input and output schemas:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph
from typing_extensions import TypedDict


# Define the schema for the input
class InputState(TypedDict):
    question: str


# Define the schema for the output
class OutputState(TypedDict):
    answer: str


# Define the overall schema, combining both input and output
class OverallState(InputState, OutputState):
    pass


# Define the node that processes the input and generates an answer
def answer_node(state: InputState):
    # Example answer and an extra key
    return {"answer": "bye", "question": state["question"]}


# Build the graph with input and output schemas specified
builder = StateGraph(OverallState, input_schema=InputState, output_schema=OutputState)
builder.add_node(answer_node)  # Add the answer node
builder.add_edge(START, "answer_node")  # Define the starting edge
builder.add_edge("answer_node", END)  # Define the ending edge
graph = builder.compile()  # Compile the graph

# Invoke the graph with an input and print the result
print(graph.invoke({"question": "hi"}))
```

```
{'answer': 'bye'}
```

Notice that the output of invoke only includes the output schema.

## Pass private state between nodes

Nodes sometimes need intermediate data that should not appear in the graph's main input or output schema. Keep that data private so only the nodes that need it can read it.

The following sequential graph has three nodes. Private data passes between the first two steps. The third step only sees the public overall state.

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph
from typing_extensions import TypedDict


# The overall state of the graph (this is the public state shared across nodes)
class OverallState(TypedDict):
    a: str


# Output from node_1 contains private data that is not part of the overall state
class Node1Output(TypedDict):
    private_data: str


# The private data is only shared between node_1 and node_2
def node_1(state: OverallState) -> Node1Output:
    output = {"private_data": "set by node_1"}
    print(f"Entered node `node_1`:\n\tInput: {state}.\n\tReturned: {output}")
    return output


# Node 2 input only requests the private data available after node_1
class Node2Input(TypedDict):
    private_data: str


def node_2(state: Node2Input) -> OverallState:
    output = {"a": "set by node_2"}
    print(f"Entered node `node_2`:\n\tInput: {state}.\n\tReturned: {output}")
    return output


# Node 3 only has access to the overall state (no access to private data from node_1)
def node_3(state: OverallState) -> OverallState:
    output = {"a": "set by node_3"}
    print(f"Entered node `node_3`:\n\tInput: {state}.\n\tReturned: {output}")
    return output


# Connect nodes in a sequence
# node_2 accepts private data from node_1, whereas
# node_3 does not see the private data.
builder = StateGraph(OverallState).add_sequence([node_1, node_2, node_3])
builder.add_edge(START, "node_1")
graph = builder.compile()

# Invoke the graph with the initial state
response = graph.invoke(
    {
        "a": "set at start",
    }
)

print()
print(f"Output of graph invocation: {response}")
```

```
Entered node `node_1`:
    Input: {'a': 'set at start'}.
    Returned: {'private_data': 'set by node_1'}
Entered node `node_2`:
    Input: {'private_data': 'set by node_1'}.
    Returned: {'a': 'set by node_2'}
Entered node `node_3`:
    Input: {'a': 'set by node_2'}.
    Returned: {'a': 'set by node_3'}

Output of graph invocation: {'a': 'set by node_3'}
```

## Private channels and streaming

Input, output, and private schemas constrain what each node reads and what `invoke` returns. They do **not** hide channels from [streaming](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/streaming.md).

Values streaming emits **all** state channels by default, including private ones. That applies to the values projection from [event streaming](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/event-streaming.md) (`stream.values` on `stream_events` / `streamEvents` with `version="v3"`), and to `graph.stream` with `stream_mode="values"` / `streamMode: "values"`. Values streaming uses the full state, not the output schema. A private channel can be missing from `invoke` output but visible in the stream.

This example uses distinct input, output, overall, and private schemas. `invoke` returns only the output schema:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from typing import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class InputState(TypedDict):
    user_input: str


class OutputState(TypedDict):
    graph_output: str


class OverallState(TypedDict):
    foo: str
    user_input: str
    graph_output: str


class PrivateState(TypedDict):
    bar: str


def node_1(state: InputState) -> OverallState:
    # Write to OverallState
    return {"foo": state["user_input"] + " name"}


def node_2(state: OverallState) -> PrivateState:
    # Read from OverallState, write to PrivateState
    return {"bar": state["foo"] + " is"}


def node_3(state: PrivateState) -> OutputState:
    # Read from PrivateState, write to OutputState
    return {"graph_output": state["bar"] + " Lance"}


builder = StateGraph(OverallState, input_schema=InputState, output_schema=OutputState)
builder.add_node("node_1", node_1)
builder.add_node("node_2", node_2)
builder.add_node("node_3", node_3)
builder.add_edge(START, "node_1")
builder.add_edge("node_1", "node_2")
builder.add_edge("node_2", "node_3")
builder.add_edge("node_3", END)

graph = builder.compile()
graph.invoke({"user_input": "My"})
# {'graph_output': 'My name is Lance'}
```

> [card] [View example trace](https://smith.langchain.com/public/370fbbfd-be69-4403-8123-716a0c2c9be6/r)
>
> Open a public LangSmith run for this example.

Streaming with the values projection still includes the private `bar` channel:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
stream = graph.stream_events({"user_input": "My"}, version="v3")
for snapshot in stream.values:
    print(snapshot)
# {'user_input': 'My'}
# {'foo': 'My name', 'user_input': 'My'}
# {'foo': 'My name', 'user_input': 'My', 'bar': 'My name is'}        # <-- private channel
# {'foo': 'My name', 'user_input': 'My', 'graph_output': 'My name is Lance', 'bar': 'My name is'}
```

> [card] [View example trace](https://smith.langchain.com/public/7d0325bb-3a2a-425f-a551-a636b297d0fb/r)
>
> Open a public LangSmith run for this example.

To restrict streamed values to specific channels (for example, only the output schema), pass `output_keys` to `graph.stream`:

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
for snapshot in graph.stream(
    {"user_input": "My"},
    stream_mode="values",
    output_keys=["graph_output"],  # [!code highlight]
):
    print(snapshot)
# {'graph_output': 'My name is Lance'}
```

If you only need the channels a node produced in each step (not the full accumulated state), use `stream_mode="updates"` instead. See [Streaming](https://docs.langchain.com/oss/python/langgraph/streaming.md).

## Use pydantic models for graph state

A [StateGraph](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langgraph/graph/state/StateGraph) accepts a [`state_schema`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langchain/middleware/#langchain.agents.middleware.AgentMiddleware.state_schema) argument that specifies the shape of the state nodes can access and update.

Most examples on this page use a Python-native `TypedDict` or [`dataclass`](https://docs.python.org/3/library/dataclasses.html) for `state_schema`. [`state_schema`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langchain/middleware/#langchain.agents.middleware.AgentMiddleware.state_schema) can be any [type](https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#type-objects).

Use a [Pydantic BaseModel](https://docs.pydantic.dev/latest/api/base_model/) for [`state_schema`](https://reference.langchain.com/python/langchain/middleware/#langchain.agents.middleware.AgentMiddleware.state_schema) when you need runtime validation on **inputs**.

> [!NOTE]
>
> **Known Limitations**
>
> * The output of the graph is **not** an instance of a Pydantic model.
> * Runtime validation only occurs on inputs to the first node in the graph, not on subsequent nodes or outputs.
> * The validation error trace from Pydantic does not show which node the error arises in.
> * Pydantic's recursive validation can be slow. For performance-sensitive applications, prefer a `dataclass`.

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph
from pydantic import BaseModel


# The overall state of the graph (this is the public state shared across nodes)
class OverallState(BaseModel):
    a: str


def node(state: OverallState):
    return {"a": "goodbye"}


# Build the state graph
builder = StateGraph(OverallState)
builder.add_node(node)  # node_1 is the first node
builder.add_edge(START, "node")  # Start the graph with node_1
builder.add_edge("node", END)  # End the graph after node_1
graph = builder.compile()

# Test the graph with a valid input
graph.invoke({"a": "hello"})
```

Invoke the graph with an **invalid** input

```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
try:
    graph.invoke({"a": 123})  # Should be a string
except Exception as e:
    print("An exception was raised because `a` is an integer rather than a string.")
    print(e)
```

```
An exception was raised because `a` is an integer rather than a string.
1 validation error for OverallState
a
  Input should be a valid string [type=string_type, input_value=123, input_type=int]
    For further information visit https://errors.pydantic.dev/2.9/v/string_type
```

See below for additional features of Pydantic model state:

> [accordion] Serialization Behavior
>
> When using Pydantic models as state schemas, serialization matters especially when:
>
> * Passing Pydantic objects as inputs
> * Receiving outputs from the graph
> * Working with nested Pydantic models
>
> The following example shows these behaviors:
>
> ```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
> from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph
> from pydantic import BaseModel
>
>
> class NestedModel(BaseModel):
>     value: str
>
>
> class ComplexState(BaseModel):
>     text: str
>     count: int
>     nested: NestedModel
>
>
> def process_node(state: ComplexState):
>     # Node receives a validated Pydantic object
>     print(f"Input state type: {type(state)}")
>     print(f"Nested type: {type(state.nested)}")
>     # Return a dictionary update
>     return {"text": state.text + " processed", "count": state.count + 1}
>
>
> # Build the graph
> builder = StateGraph(ComplexState)
> builder.add_node("process", process_node)
> builder.add_edge(START, "process")
> builder.add_edge("process", END)
> graph = builder.compile()
>
> # Create a Pydantic instance for input
> input_state = ComplexState(text="hello", count=0, nested=NestedModel(value="test"))
> print(f"Input object type: {type(input_state)}")
>
> # Invoke graph with a Pydantic instance
> result = graph.invoke(input_state)
> print(f"Output type: {type(result)}")
> print(f"Output content: {result}")
>
> # Convert back to Pydantic model if needed
> output_model = ComplexState(**result)
> print(f"Converted back to Pydantic: {type(output_model)}")
> ```

> [accordion] Runtime Type Coercion
>
> Pydantic performs runtime type coercion for certain data types. That can help, but it can also produce unexpected values if you do not account for it.
>
> ```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
> from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph
> from pydantic import BaseModel
>
>
> class CoercionExample(BaseModel):
>     # Pydantic will coerce string numbers to integers
>     number: int
>     # Pydantic will parse string booleans to bool
>     flag: bool
>
>
> def inspect_node(state: CoercionExample):
>     print(f"number: {state.number} (type: {type(state.number)})")
>     print(f"flag: {state.flag} (type: {type(state.flag)})")
>     return {}
>
>
> builder = StateGraph(CoercionExample)
> builder.add_node("inspect", inspect_node)
> builder.add_edge(START, "inspect")
> builder.add_edge("inspect", END)
> graph = builder.compile()
>
> # Demonstrate coercion with string inputs that will be converted
> result = graph.invoke({"number": "42", "flag": "true"})
>
> # This would fail with a validation error
> try:
>     graph.invoke({"number": "not-a-number", "flag": "true"})
> except Exception as e:
>     print(f"\nExpected validation error: {e}")
> ```

> [accordion] Working with Message Models
>
> When working with LangChain message types in a state schema, serialization matters. Use `AnyMessage` (rather than `BaseMessage`) for proper serialization and deserialization when message objects travel over the wire.
>
> ```python theme={"theme":{"light":"catppuccin-latte","dark":"catppuccin-mocha"}}
> from typing import List
>
> from langchain.messages import AIMessage, AnyMessage, HumanMessage
> from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph
> from pydantic import BaseModel
>
>
> class ChatState(BaseModel):
>     messages: List[AnyMessage]
>     context: str
>
>
> def add_message(state: ChatState):
>     return {"messages": state.messages + [AIMessage(content="Hello there!")]}
>
>
> builder = StateGraph(ChatState)
> builder.add_node("add_message", add_message)
> builder.add_edge(START, "add_message")
> builder.add_edge("add_message", END)
> graph = builder.compile()
>
> # Create input with a message
> initial_state = ChatState(
>     messages=[HumanMessage(content="Hi")], context="Customer support chat"
> )
>
> result = graph.invoke(initial_state)
> print(f"Output: {result}")
>
> # Convert back to Pydantic model to see message types
> output_model = ChatState(**result)
> for i, msg in enumerate(output_model.messages):
>     print(f"Message {i}: {type(msg).__name__} - {msg.content}")
> ```
